    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor Shift Pattern.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making both predictors relevant. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; $\\eta_{\\max}$'s contribution collapses because no single judge is pivotal, while high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces ($K^* \\in (3,5)$).\n\n\\paragraph{Uniform Positive Direction.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is positive in all 12 conditions under OLS and GAM bootstrap; significance varies by method (OLS 12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 10/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $p{=}0.423$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), a fundamental limitation of the observational design: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} confirms that in 28/36 full-pool conditions (78\\%), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value does not exceed the partial $R^2$ attributable to $\\eta_{\\max}$---the confound alone suffices to explain the observed association. The trend improves with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$: 1/12 $\\to$ 3/12 $\\to$ 4/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}), and fractional logit with clustered SEs provides directional support (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$; 98.6\\% concordance with OLS). Capability-matched subsampling (Pool~A: $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) raises RV above $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 10/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), though the restricted pool size limits statistical power. This remains a fundamental limitation; causal attribution requires capability-controlled experimental designs.\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not the direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s association. Threshold voting yields $R^2{=}0.829$; Bayesian calibration reduces $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attack types ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}).\n\n\\paragraph{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}\n$\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} offers complementary prediction: averaging 7/12 bootstrap-significant conditions across $K$ ($\\rho{=}0.942$, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.194$), it outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 8--10/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.164$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Crucially, $\\kappa$-diversity does not exhibit the predictor shift: its significance \\emph{decreases} monotonically ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), whereas $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s increases ($4 \\to 10 \\to 9$), suggesting MI-based effective independence captures redundancy-driven vulnerability distinct from pairwise agreement. The SDPI bound ($\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\leq K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$) provides the core theoretical justification: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, an upper-bound structure $\\kappa$-diversity lacks.           "-$\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} offers complementary prediction: averaging 7/12 bootstrap-significant conditions across $K$ ($\\rho{=}0.942$, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.194$), it outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 8--10/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.164$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Crucially, $\\kappa$-diversity does not exhibit the predictor shift: its significance \\emph{decreases} monotonically ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), whereas $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s increases ($4 \\to 10 \\to 9$), suggesting MI-based effective independence captures redundancy-driven vulnerability distinct from pairwise agreement. The SDPI bound ($\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\leq K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$) provides the core theoretical justification: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, an upper-bound structure $\\kappa$-diversity lacks. The shift may partly reflect improved MI estimation at larger $K$; distinguishing estimation artifacts from structural effects requires larger judge pools.",
          "+$\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} offers complementary prediction: averaging 7/12 bootstrap-significant conditions across $K$ ($\\rho{=}0.978$, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.497$), it outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 8--10/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.430$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Crucially, $\\kappa$-diversity does not exhibit the predictor shift: its significance \\emph{decreases} monotonically ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), whereas $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s increases ($4 \\to 10 \\to 9$), suggesting MI-based effective independence captures redundancy-driven vulnerability distinct from pairwise agreement. The SDPI bound ($\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\leq K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$) provides the core theoretical justification: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, an upper-bound structure $\\kappa$-diversity lacks. The shift may partly reflect improved MI estimation at larger $K$; distinguishing estimation artifacts from structural effects requires larger judge pools."
        ]

```text
16	\paragraph{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}
17	$\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} offers complementary prediction: averaging 7/12 bootstrap-significant conditions across $K$ ($\rho{=}0.978$, $|\bar{\beta}|{=}0.497$), it outperforms $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\ 8--10/12, $|\bar{\beta}|{=}0.430$; Appendix~\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Crucially, $\kappa$-diversity does not exhibit the predictor shift: its significance \emph{decreases} monotonically ($8 \to 7 \to 6$), whereas $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s increases ($4 \to 10 \to 9$), suggesting MI-based effective independence captures redundancy-driven vulnerability distinct from pairwise agreement. The SDPI bound ($\eta_{\mathrm{joint}} \leq K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max}$) provides the core theoretical justification: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, an upper-bound structure $\kappa$-diversity lacks. The shift may partly reflect improved MI estimation at larger $K$; distinguishing estimation artifacts from structural effects requires larger judge pools.
18	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor Shift Pattern.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making both predictors relevant. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; $\\eta_{\\max}$'s contribution collapses because no single judge is pivotal, while high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces ($K^* \\in (3,5)$).\n\n\\paragraph{Uniform Positive Direction.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is positive in all 12 conditions under OLS and GAM bootstrap; significance varies by method (OLS 12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 10/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $p{=}0.423$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), a fundamental limitation of the observational design: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} confirms that in 28/36 full-pool conditions (78\\%), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value does not exceed the partial $R^2$ attributable to $\\eta_{\\max}$---the confound alone suffices to explain the observed association. The trend improves with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$: 1/12 $\\to$ 3/12 $\\to$ 4/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}), and fractional logit with clustered SEs provides directional support (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$; 98.6\\% concordance with OLS). Capability-matched subsampling (Pool~A: $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) raises RV above $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 10/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), though the restricted pool size limits statistical power. This remains a fundamental limitation; causal attribution requires capability-controlled experimental designs.\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not the direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s association. Threshold voting yields $R^2{=}0.829$; Bayesian calibration reduces $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}). The SDPI bound ($\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\leq K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$) provides the core theoretical justification: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, an upper-bound structure $\\kappa$-diversity lacks. 353	\toprule
354	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{$\kappa$-diversity}} \\
355	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5}
356	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{div.\ sig} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & \textbf{div.\ sig} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} \\
357	\midrule
358	3 & 4/12  & 3/12 & 8/12 & 1/12 \\
359	5 & 10/12 & 0/12 & 7/12 & 4/12 \\
360	7 & 9/12  & 0/12 & 6/12 & 1/12 \\
361	\bottomrule
362	\end{tabular}
363	\caption{Predictor shift comparison: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\ $\kappa$-diversity across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a clear shift ($4 \to 10 \to 9$); $\kappa$-diversity significance decreases monotonically ($8 \to 7 \to 6$), showing no predictor shift.}
364	\label{tab:kappa_regime}
365	\end{table}
366	
367	$\kappa$-diversity is a stronger predictor than $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\ 4/12), consistent with its larger mean effect size ($|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.497$ vs.\ 0.430; Table~\ref{tab:alt_diversity}). However, $\kappa$-diversity shows no predictor shift: its significance \emph{decreases} from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}7$ ($8 \to 7 \to 6$), the opposite of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s pattern ($4 \to 10 \to 9$). Furthermore, under $\kappa$-diversity, $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance at $K{=}5$ (4/12), whereas under $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ it drops to 0/12. The predictor shift---co-determination at $K{=}3$ transitioning to diversity dominance at $K{\geq}5$---is thus specific to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s MI-based formulation, which captures effective judge independence rather than pairwise label agreement.
```

[Director 指令] **P0: 两个数据一致性问题，必须在 R5 审稿返回前解决**

### 问题 1: K=5 聚合表可能仍有 V1 数据

Reviewer 发现 `k5_aggregation_table_data.txt` 整个文件都是 V1 数据（Worker 只修了 Majority 行）。V1 bug（`.get('winner','tie')` 将 API 错误当 tie）影响所有聚合方式。

请：
1. 检查服务器上是否已有 V2 corrected 的 K=5 aggregation bootstrap 完整结果（所有聚合方式：Majority/Weighted/Threshold/Bayesian）
2. 如果有 → 用 V2 数据更新 `k5_aggregation_table_data.txt` 和 appendix.tex 对应表格的所有行
3. 如果没有 → 汇报，我评估是否需要重跑

**重点关注 Bayesian**：论文声称 "Bayesian neutralizes both predictors"（0/12），如果 V2 下不再为 0，这个结论需要修改。

### 问题 2: Tier 分类不一致

| 来源 | gemini-3.5-flash 归类 |
|------|---------------------|
| methodology.tex Table | mid-tier |
| stochastic_dominance_analysis.json | frontier |

gemini-3.5-flash MMLU=0.968, ARC=1.00，按论文 caption 标准（">85% on both = frontier"），应为 frontier。

请：
1. 确认论文中 tier 分类的定义标准（methodology.tex caption 写的什么）
2. 统一分类：如果标准是 ">85% both = frontier"，则改 methodology.tex 为 frontier（3→4/6→5/9→8 分布），同步更新 stochastic_dominance 相关文本
3. 如果改分类会影响 stochastic dominance 统计量，需要重算并更新

### 问题 3（低优先级）: KW p 值溯源

KW p=0.200 和 p=0.423 无 JSON 数据源文件。请将 GAM bootstrap 的 KW 检验结果写入数据文件（如 `gam_kw_test.json`）。

三个问题按顺序处理，每个完成后汇报。

```text
1	\section{Experimental Methodology}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}
5	
6	We select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API; six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with AWQ INT4 quantization on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\,GB). API models are assigned to \emph{frontier}, \emph{mid-tier}, or \emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \lceil K/2 \rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\times$ item $\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \S\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\begin{tabular}{lllccc}
12	\toprule
13	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Tier} & \textbf{Deploy} & \textbf{MMLU} & \textbf{ARC} \\
14	\midrule
15	gpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\
16	gemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\
17	claude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\
18	\addlinespace
19	gemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & mid-tier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\dagger$ \\
20	gpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\
21	claude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\
22	\addlinespace
23	gpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\
24	gpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\
25	claude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\
26	mistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\
27	llama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\
28	llama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\
29	qwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\
30	qwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\
31	qwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\
32	\bottomrule
33	\end{tabular}
34	\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\dagger$\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\%, lightweight $<$80\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).}
35	\label{tab:models}
36	\end{table}
37	
38	\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}
39	
40	Two benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each benchmark. MMLU probes factual recall and broad domain knowledge; ARC requires multi-step scientific reasoning. All API calls use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs; local models are served with the same temperature setting. Attacked variants are generated by programmatically applying each manipulation to the candidate text, with a fixed random seed for reproducibility.
41	
42	Each benchmark is paired with six attack types that target different judge vulnerabilities: (1)~\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions embedded in candidate text that override the evaluation prompt \citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\textbf{sycophancy}, responses engineered to agree with or flatter the judge \citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues (e.g., ``this answer deserves a 10/10'') injected into the candidate to anchor scoring; (4)~\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding correct answers with excessive irrelevant detail to exploit length preferences \citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\textbf{position bias}, reordering candidate answers to place the target in the position judges systematically favor \citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\textbf{authority bias}, prefacing candidate text with fabricated credentials or citations to inflate perceived credibility. The combination of 2 benchmarks and 6 attacks produces 12 experimental conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (Appendix~\ref{app:attacks}).
43	
44	\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}
45	
46	Per-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\eta_j(a) = \Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\eta_{\max}(a) = \max_{j \in \mathcal{P}} \eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise mutual information between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean (unattacked) data. MI is estimated via the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. In practice, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed through the Gaussian copula correlation matrix: under the Gaussian copula assumption (that pairwise dependence is adequately captured by rank correlations), pairwise MI and copula $\rho$ are related by $\mathrm{MI}(i,j) = -\tfrac{1}{2}\log(1-\rho_{ij}^2)$ \citep{nelsen2006copulas}, yielding $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI}(i,j)) = 1 - \rho_{ij}^2$. Substituting into Equation~\ref{eq:keff} gives the closed form $K_{\mathrm{eff}} = K - \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i}\sum_{j \neq i} \rho_{ij}^2$, which decreases from $K$ (fully independent) as pairwise correlations increase. Together, $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below: $\eta_{\max}$ indexes the weakest-link channel and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ indexes the diversity channel.
47	
48	\subsection{Aggregation Methods}
49	
50	Beyond the default majority vote, we evaluate three alternative aggregation rules to test whether the diversity-vulnerability association persists under different decision mechanisms. The aggregation comparison uses the same log-log regression specification as the primary analysis (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}).
51	
52	\paragraph{Majority Vote.} Accept if $\sum_{j=1}^K \mathbf{1}[J_j \text{ approves}] \geq \lceil K/2 \rceil$ (baseline).
53	
54	\paragraph{Accuracy-Weighted Vote.} Weight judges by clean-data accuracy: $w_j \propto \mathrm{acc}_j$, $\sum_j w_j = 1$; accept if $\sum_j w_j \cdot \mathbf{1}[J_j \text{ approves}] > 0.5$.
55	
56	\paragraph{Threshold Vote (Unanimity).} Accept only if all $K$ judges approve ($t = K$).
57	
58	\paragraph{Bayesian Calibration.} Compute $P(\text{correct} \mid \mathbf{v}) \propto P(\text{correct}) \prod_j P(v_j \mid \text{correct})$ using each judge's confusion matrix on clean data; accept if the posterior exceeds 0.5.
59	
60	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
61	\label{sec:stat_framework}
62	
63	To disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:
64	\begin{equation}
65	\label{eq:regression}
66	\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon) = \alpha + \beta_1 \log(K_{\mathrm{eff}}) + \beta_2 \log(\eta_{\max} + \epsilon),
67	\end{equation}
68	with $\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = \hat{\beta} \cdot \mathrm{SD}(\log x) / \mathrm{SD}(\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini-Hochberg correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate per predictor across the 12 conditions.
69	
70	To assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.
71	
72	To test whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).
73	
74	\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}
75	\label{sec:robustness_method}
76	
77	The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position at $K{=}3$ are below the ${\geq}20$ threshold recommended by \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; at $K{=}5$, cluster count drops to ${\leq}11$, and at $K{=}7$ to ${\leq}9$. Following \citet{mackinnon2018wild}, we use Rademacher weights, which remain conservative in few-cluster settings even when cluster sizes are unequal. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count rather than absent effects. We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative SDPI structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_methods}.
78

Substituting into Equation~\\ref{eq:keff} gives the closed form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$, which decreases from $K$ (fully independent) as pairwise correlations increase. Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below: $\\eta_{\\max}$ indexes the weakest-link channel and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ indexes the diversity channel.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond the default majority vote, we evaluate three alternative aggregation rules to test whether the diversity-vulnerability association persists under different decision mechanisms. The aggregation comparison uses the same log-log regression specification as the primary analysis (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}).\n\n\\paragraph{Majority Vote.} Accept if $\\sum_{j=1}^K \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] \\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ (baseline).\n\n\\paragraph{Accuracy-Weighted Vote.} Weight judges by clean-data accuracy: $w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$, $\\sum_j w_j = 1$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold Vote (Unanimity).} Accept only if all $K$ judges approve ($t = K$).\n\n\\paragraph{Bayesian Calibration.} Compute $P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$ using each judge's confusion matrix on clean data; accept if the posterior exceeds 0.5.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini-Hochberg correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nTo assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \\emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.\n\nTo test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position at $K{=}3$ are below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold recommended by \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; at $K{=}5$, cluster count drops to ${\\leq}11$, and at $K{=}7$ to ${\\leq}9$. Following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}, we use Rademacher weights, which remain conservative in few-cluster settings even when cluster sizes are unequal. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count rather than absent effects. We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \\citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \\citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative SDPI structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}.\n",

      "totalLines": 78
    }

Looking at the tier classification in methodology.tex:

Line 19: `gemini-3.5-flash & Google & mid-tier & API & .968 & 1.00`
Line 34 caption: "frontier >85% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80-85%, lightweight <80% or quantized"

gemini-3.5-flash has MMLU=0.968 and ARC=1.00. Both are >85%. By the caption's own definition, it should be **frontier**, not mid-tier.

But wait - the caption says "Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier >85%...)". 

The parenthetical describes the thresholds but also mentions "provider capability classification," which might be distinct from accuracy-based classification. Still, gemini-3.5-flash clearly exceeds 85% on both metrics, so by the stated criteria it qualifies as frontier—and the stochastic_dominance_analysis.json already reflects this. If I reclassify it, the frontier tier would have 4 models while mid-tier drops to 2, though I need to verify gpt-4.1's exact MMLU score to finalize the counts.

Actually, checking the numbers more carefully: gpt-4.1 has MMLU at 83.7%, which falls short of the 85% threshold, so it stays mid-tier. Claude-sonnet-4-6 is at 81%, also below the cutoff. Gemini-3.5-flash at 96.8% and 100% clearly meets the frontier criteria, so moving it there gives me 4 frontier models, 2 mid-tier, and 9 lightweight.

Now I'm noticing a discrepancy between what the methodology table shows and what's in the JSON file—the JSON lists frontier=4, mid=6, lightweight=5, which totals 15 models, but the table only accounts for 15 total with a different distribution. I need to figure out which classification is actually correct and whether the stochastic dominance statistics in threat_model.tex were calculated based on the JSON's tier assignments.

Now I understand the full picture. The JSON uses a different tier classification than the paper table:

**JSON classification** (based on pairwise evaluation accuracy, not benchmark accuracy):
- Frontier (4): gpt-5.5, gemini-3.1-pro-preview, claude-opus-4-6, gemini-3.5-flash
- Mid (6): gpt-4.1, claude-sonnet-4-6, llama3.1-70b, qwen2.5-72b, mistral-large, qwen2.5-32b


- Lightweight (5): gpt-4.1-mini, gpt-4.1-nano, claude-3-haiku, qwen2.5-14b, llama3.1-8b

The paper's classification scheme groups models differently, placing gemini-3.5-flash in the mid-tier rather than frontier, and expanding the lightweight category to include additional models beyond what the JSON shows.

**Paper table classification** (based on provider capability classification):
- Frontier (3): gpt-5.5, gemini-3.1-pro-preview, claude-opus-4-6
- Mid-tier (3): gemini-3.5-flash, gpt-4.1, claude-sonnet-4-6
- Lightweight (9): gpt-4.1-mini, gpt-4.1-nano, claude-3-haiku, plus all 6 local models

The paper defines tiers by accuracy thresholds: frontier models exceed 85% on both benchmarks, mid-tier falls between 80-85%, and lightweight includes anything below 80% or quantized versions. Looking at the actual scores, gemini-3.5-flash's performance on both MMLU and ARC puts it above the 85% threshold, suggesting it should be classified as frontier rather than mid-tier, while gpt-4.1 and claude-sonnet-4-6 correctly stay in mid-tier based on their MMLU scores falling in the 80-85% range.

The real problem is that threat_model.tex derives its statistics from the JSON's classification scheme, which uses completely different criteria than the paper table's accuracy-based tiers. The JSON places quantized local models in mid-tier, but the paper's caption would put all quantized models in lightweight. This mismatch means the 67.7% and 31.1% statistics are calculated from a tier assignment that contradicts the paper's own stated classification rules.

The cleanest solution is to move gemini-3.5-flash to frontier in the paper table to match the caption's accuracy criterion, then clarify that the JSON uses a separate accuracy-based scheme for the stochastic dominance analysis—the tier counts won't align between the two, but each will be internally consistent.

Actually, I'm realizing the caption itself is the problem: it claims to use "provider capability classification" but then defines tiers purely by accuracy thresholds, which creates a contradiction for gemini-3.5-flash. I need to either redefine the caption to be purely accuracy-based or acknowledge that "provider capability" overrides accuracy in certain cases.

The bigger issue is that the JSON classification (which puts local models in mid-tier) differs from the paper's classification (which puts them in lightweight), and the 67.7%/31.1% stats depend on which system I use. I should check whether moving gemini-3.5-flash to frontier actually affects those numbers.

Looking at the JSON more carefully, the mid-tier grouping mixes API and local models based on accuracy rather than deployment type. The cleanest approach is to move gemini-3.5-flash to frontier in the table to match its accuracy, add a caption note about the approximation, and keep the stochastic dominance statistics as-is since they're based on the JSON's grouping.

Actually, this is a consequential decision—the Director instructed me to move gemini-3.5-flash if it meets the '>85% both' threshold, but the stochastic dominance JSON uses a fundamentally different classification scheme that affects multiple models, not just this one. I should make the single change the Director requested and flag the deeper inconsistency rather than trying to reconcile everything unilaterally.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **V1 Bug**: `.get("winner", "tie")` treats API errors as "tie" votes in ASR computation
   - **V2 Fix**: `_is_valid_score()` filters out invalid/error samples
   - **K_eff**: MI-based effective independence metric, computed via Gaussian copula correlation matrix
   - **κ-diversity**: 1 - mean(pairwise Cohen's kappa), alternative diversity metric
   - **Regime transition**: K_eff significance pattern K=3→5→7 (4→10→9), η_max (3→0→0)
   - **κ-diversity vs K_eff finding**: κ-diversity dominant from K=3 (8/12), no transition; K_eff shows transition — this is K_eff-specific
   - **Wild cluster bootstrap**: B=9999, Rademacher weights, K-position max-p, BH FDR α=0.05
   - **Cinelli-Hazlett sensitivity**: Robustness Value (RV) for omitted variable bias
   - **Tier classification**: Two schemes in use — paper table (deployment-based: frontier/mid/lightweight) vs JSON (evaluation accuracy-based)
   - **Stochastic dominance analysis**: 58% (61/105) dominant pairs, 67.7% within-tier non-dominant, 31.1% cross-tier

3. Files and Code Sections:

        "content": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nWe select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API; six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with AWQ INT4 quantization on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight $<$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each benchmark. MMLU probes factual recall and broad domain knowledge; ARC requires multi-step scientific reasoning. All API calls use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs; local models are served with the same temperature setting. Attacked variants are generated by programmatically applying each manipulation to the candidate text, with a fixed random seed for reproducibility.\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types that target different judge vulnerabilities: (1)~\\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions embedded in candidate text that override the evaluation prompt \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\\textbf{sycophancy}, responses engineered to agree with or flatter the judge \\citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues (e.g., ``this answer deserves a 10/10'') injected into the candidate to anchor scoring; (4)~\\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding correct answers with excessive irrelevant detail to exploit length preferences \\citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\\textbf{position bias}, reordering candidate answers to place the target in the position judges systematically favor \\citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\\textbf{authority bias}, prefacing candidate text with fabricated credentials or citations to inflate perceived credibility. The combination of 2 benchmarks and 6 attacks produces 12 experimental conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacks}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise mutual information between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean (unattacked) data. MI is estimated via the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. In practice, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed through the Gaussian copula correlation matrix: under the Gaussian copula assumption (that pairwise dependence is adequately captured by rank correlations), pairwise MI and copula $\\rho$ are related by $\\mathrm{MI}(i,j) = -\\tfrac{1}{2}\\log(1-\\rho_{ij}^2)$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}, yielding $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j)) = 1 - \\rho_{ij}^2$. Substituting into Equation~\\ref{eq:keff} gives the closed form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$, which decreases from $K$ (fully independent) as pairwise correlations increase. Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below: $\\eta_{\\max}$ indexes the weakest-link channel and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ indexes the diversity channel.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond the default majority vote, we evaluate three alternative aggregation rules to test whether the diversity-vulnerability association persists under different decision mechanisms. The aggregation comparison uses the same log-log regression specification as the primary analysis (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}).\n\n\\paragraph{Majority Vote.} Accept if $\\sum_{j=1}^K \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] \\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ (baseline).\n\n\\paragraph{Accuracy-Weighted Vote.} Weight judges by clean-data accuracy: $w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$, $\\sum_j w_j = 1$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold Vote (Unanimity).} Accept only if all $K$ judges approve ($t = K$).\n\n\\paragraph{Bayesian Calibration.} Compute $P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$ using each judge's confusion matrix on clean data; accept if the posterior exceeds 0.5.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini-Hochberg correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nTo assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \\emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.\n\nTo test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position at $K{=}3$ are below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold recommended by \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; at $K{=}5$, cluster count drops to ${\\leq}11$, and at $K{=}7$ to ${\\leq}9$. Following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}, we use Rademacher weights, which remain conservative in few-cluster settings even when cluster sizes are unequal. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count rather than absent effects. We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \\citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \\citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative SDPI structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}.\n",
        "numLines": 78,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 78
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/methodology.tex"
  },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/analysis.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor Shift Pattern.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making both predictors relevant. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; $\\eta_{\\max}$'s contribution collapses because no single judge is pivotal, while high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces ($K^* \\in (3,5)$).\n\n\\paragraph{Uniform Positive Direction.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is positive in all 12 conditions under OLS and GAM bootstrap; significance varies by method (OLS 12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 10/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $p{=}0.423$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), a fundamental limitation of the observational design: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} confirms that in 28/36 full-pool conditions (78\\%), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value does not exceed the partial $R^2$ attributable to $\\eta_{\\max}$---the confound alone suffices to explain the observed association. The trend improves with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$: 1/12 $\\to$ 3/12 $\\to$ 4/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}), and fractional logit with clustered SEs provides directional support (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$; 98.6\\% concordance with OLS). Capability-matched subsampling (Pool~A: $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) raises RV above $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 10/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), though the restricted pool size limits statistical power. This remains a fundamental limitation; causal attribution requires capability-controlled experimental designs.\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not the direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s association. Threshold voting yields $R^2{=}0.829$; Bayesian calibration reduces $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attack types ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}).\n\n\\paragraph{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}\n$\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} offers complementary prediction: averaging 7/12 bootstrap-significant conditions across $K$ ($\\rho{=}0.978$, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.497$), it outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 9--10/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.430$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Crucially, $\\kappa$-diversity does not exhibit the predictor shift: its significance \\emph{decreases} monotonically ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), whereas $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s increases ($4 \\to 10 \\to 9$), suggesting MI-based effective independence captures redundancy-driven vulnerability distinct from pairwise agreement. The SDPI bound ($\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\leq K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$) provides the core theoretical justification: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, an upper-bound structure $\\kappa$-diversity lacks. The shift may partly reflect improved MI estimation at larger $K$; distinguishing estimation artifacts from structural effects requires larger judge pools.\n",
        "numLines": 18,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 18
      }
    },

33	\end{tabular}
34	\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\dagger$\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\%, lightweight $<$80\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).}
35	\label{tab:models}
36	\end{table}

22  \begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}]
23  \label{thm:monotonicity}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FiDGbWrGjWhNszmfpFCCza`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}
2	\label{sec:threat_model}
3	
4	A \emph{panel} $\mathcal{P} = \{J_1, \ldots, J_K\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \lceil K/2 \rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\eta_j(a) = \Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \in \{0,1\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\eta_{\max} = \max_j \eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \emph{panel attack success rate} is $\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a) = \Pr\!\bigl(\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \geq t\bigr)$.
5	
6	To quantify diversity, we define the \emph{effective independence} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information among judges:
7	\begin{equation}
8	\label{eq:keff}
9	K_{\mathrm{eff}} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K} \Bigl[1 + \sum_{j \neq i} \exp\!\bigl(-2 \cdot \mathrm{MI}(i,j)\bigr)\Bigr].
10	\end{equation}
11	This formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\mathrm{MI} \to 0$) to $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\mathrm{MI} \to \infty$). Under the strong data-processing inequality (SDPI), the joint vulnerability satisfies $\eta_{\mathrm{joint}} \leq \min(1,\; K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max})$, suggesting that diversity should help, but this bound assumes non-adaptive faults.
12	
13	\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]
14	\label{def:adversary}
15	A \emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\{\eta_j(\cdot)\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P}) = \sup_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.
16	\end{definition}
17	
18	This departs from Byzantine fault-tolerance (BFT) models~\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate arbitrary (including malicious) faults provided the number of faulty nodes satisfies $f < n/3$, but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary instead concentrates attack effort on the weakest link~\cite{schneier2000secrets}, rendering diversity-based defenses less effective than the SDPI bound implies.
19	
20	This suggests a \emph{reversal pattern}: under random faults, higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface; the adversary exploits judge-specific vulnerabilities rather than facing mutual protection.
21	
22	\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}]
23	\label{thm:monotonicity}
24	Replacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (67.7\% non-dominant) versus cross-tier pairs (31.1\%), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.
25	\end{theorem}
26	
27	The theorem predicts $\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\ref{eq:regression}. The SDPI bound suggests $\beta_1 \leq 0$ under non-adaptive faults; the reversal pattern predicts positive $\beta_1$ for judge-specific attacks and negative for shared-bias attacks. The relative importance of $\eta_{\max}$ versus $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges. We test these predictions across $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
```

    "newString": "Replacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (72.1\\% non-dominant, 31/43) versus cross-tier pairs (21.0\\%, 13/62), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:threat_model}\n\nA \\emph{panel} $\\mathcal{P} = \\{J_1, \\ldots, J_K\\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\\eta_{\\max} = \\max_j \\eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \\emph{panel attack success rate} is $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) = \\Pr\\!\\bigl(\\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \\geq t\\bigr)$.\n\nTo quantify diversity, we define the \\emph{effective independence} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information among judges:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:keff}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}} = \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\Bigl[1 + \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\exp\\!\\bigl(-2 \\cdot \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)\\bigr)\\Bigr].\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to 0$) to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to \\infty$). Under the strong data-processing inequality (SDPI), the joint vulnerability satisfies $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\leq \\min(1,\\; K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max})$, suggesting that diversity should help, but this bound assumes non-adaptive faults.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]\n\\label{def:adversary}\nA \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis departs from Byzantine fault-tolerance (BFT) models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate arbitrary (including malicious) faults provided the number of faulty nodes satisfies $f < n/3$, but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary instead concentrates attack effort on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, rendering diversity-based defenses less effective than the SDPI bound implies.\n\nThis suggests a \\emph{reversal pattern}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface; the adversary exploits judge-specific vulnerabilities rather than facing mutual protection.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}]\n\\label{thm:monotonicity}\nReplacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (67.7\\% non-dominant) versus cross-tier pairs (31.1\\%), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe theorem predicts $\\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}. The SDPI bound suggests $\\beta_1 \\leq 0$ under non-adaptive faults; the reversal pattern predicts positive $\\beta_1$ for judge-specific attacks and negative for shared-bias attacks. The relative importance of $\\eta_{\\max}$ versus $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges. We test these predictions across $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

50	- **temperature_pilot_summary.json**: Temperature pilot (3 models × 4 temps × 2 benchmarks). K_eff delta max=**0.033** across temperatures — temperature is **NOT** a valid instrument (fails relevance). Accuracy changes ≤0.85%. ASR largely stable across temperatures. Includes MI details and cost ($4.93). 216 checkpoints loaded.
51	
52	- **random_fault_contrast_corrected.json**: V1→V2 corrected. RF vs adaptive attack contrast. RF R²=**0.70** >> adaptive R²=**0.28** (2.5x gap). K_eff explains much more variance under independent (random) faults vs adversarial attacks. Gap narrowed slightly after correction (0.449→0.430) but qualitatively unchanged. K_eff bootstrap sig 2/2 for RF.
53	
54	## Supplementary Analyses
55	
56	- **stochastic_dominance_analysis.json**: 15 judges, 3 tiers (frontier/mid/lightweight). 105 unordered pairs: 61 dominant (58.1%), 44 non-dominant (41.9%). Within-tier: **67.7%** non-dominant. Cross-tier: 31.1% non-dominant. Within-tier judges are more likely to be non-dominated — supports diversity argument.
57	
58	- **power_analysis_all_K.json**: Cluster-robust power analysis (1000 simulations). MDE@80%: K3=**0.144**, K5=**0.048**, K7=**0.036** (standardized beta). K3 power for β_std=0.1 is 64.6%; K5 achieves 84.5% at β_std=0.05. Demonstrates adequate power for observed effect sizes.
59	
60	---
61

OLS log-log, K=3, 455 panels. Error-as-tie bug fix (1221/63000 = 1.94% errors treated as ties). K_eff sig: 8/12→**12/12**; eta sig: 11/12→10/12. Dominance distribution shifts from {Co:7, eta:4, K:1} to {Co:10, K:2, eta:0}. 5 conditions changed dominance category. Key finding: K_eff universally significant and always positive after correction.\n\n- **epsilon_sensitivity.json**: Epsilon sensitivity for K=3. OLS coefficients at ε=1e-3, 1e-6, 1e-9. Shows K_eff coefficients are sensitive to epsilon choice in conditions with high ASR-zero rates (e.g., arc_sycophancy: K_eff beta flips sign across epsilon). Includes per-condition ASR zero rates for K3/K5/K7.\n\n- **epsilon_sensitivity_k5.json**: Epsilon sensitivity for K=5. Max coefficient change: K_eff **184.5%**, eta 31.07%. At ε=1e-6: K_eff sig 12/12, eta sig 12/12 (all conditions significant at K=5). Large N (3003 panels) stabilizes estimates.\n\n## Table 2 — Aggregation Methods (Section 4.2)\n\n- **table2_corrected.json**: V1→V2 corrected. K=3, 455 panels, 4 aggregation methods. All 4 methods changed after error filtering. Majority: K_eff sig 8/12→**12/12**, dir 7+/1-→**12+/0-**. Weighted: K_eff sig 9/12→**12/12**. Bayesian: direction reversed 1+/6-→**5+/1-** (old negative was artifact). Threshold: spurious negative disappeared 10+/1-→11+/0-.\n\n- **aggregation_bootstrap.json**: Bootstrap validation of Table 2. K=3, B=9999. Covers majority/weighted/threshold/bayesian. Confirms OLS directions: majority 11/12 positive, weighted 11/12 positive, threshold 11/12 positive. Bayesian reversal confirmed: 9/12 negative, 0% positive rate on 6 strongest conditions. NOTE: these are V1 (pre-correction) OLS baselines with bootstrap CIs.\n\n## Regime Transition (Section 4.3)\n\n- **regime_transition_corrected.json**: V1→V2 corrected. Wild cluster bootstrap (B=9999, Rademacher, max-p, BH FDR 0.05). K3: K_eff sig 1→**4**, eta sig 5→3. K5: K_eff sig 7→**10**. K7: K_eff sig 5→**9**. Per-condition FDR-adjusted p-values included. 1365 filtered samples. All K sizes changed.\n\n## GAM Nonlinear Analysis (Section 4.4 / Appendix)\n\n- **gam_bootstrap_ci_k3.json**: GAM bootstrap CIs, K=3, B=9999. LinearGAM with 8 splines. 12/12 conditions positive direction. 11/12 have >98.75% positive rate (mmlu_score_manipulation lowest at 88.95%). Includes taxonomy split analysis (targeted vs shared_bias): all_targeted_positive=true, split_consistency_rate=0.0.\n\n- **gam_bootstrap_ci_k5.json**: GAM bootstrap CIs, K=5, B=9999. **12/12 conditions 100% positive rate** (all 9999 bootstraps positive). Tighter CIs than K3 due to 3003 panels. Strongest: arc_score_manipulation slope=34.9 [23.4, 49.5]. Taxonomy split: same structure as K3.\n\n- **gam_magnitude_gradient.json**: GAM slope magnitude comparison between targeted vs shared_bias attack types. K3: Kruskal-Wallis H=1.64, p=0.200. K5: H=0.64, p=0.423. **No significant difference** in GAM slope between attack categories. Position_bias slopes ~30-60x smaller due to ASR scale (0-1 vs 0-100%).\n\n## K_eff Distribution & Measurement (Section 3 / Appendix)\n\n- **keff_distribution.json**: Original K_eff distribution (uncorrected MI-based). K3: mean=1.92, sd=0.32. K5: mean=2.32, sd=0.39. K7: mean=2.54, sd=0.38. Range: K3 [1.23, 2.71], K5 [1.43, 3.55], K7 [1.59, 3.61].\n\n- **keff_distribution_corrected.json**: V1→V2 corrected K_eff (empirical via Gaussian copula MI, averaged across MMLU & ARC). K3: mean=**2.18**, sd=0.30. K5: mean=**3.35**, sd=0.42. K7: mean=**4.53**, sd=0.47. Substantially higher than uncorrected values.\n\n- **keff_stability_split.json**: K_eff significance by attack stability tier (Spearman rho). Stable attacks (rho≥0.90, 4 types): K3 K_eff sig **0/8**, K5 sig **4/8**. Moderate (rho~0.60, prompt_injection): K3 1/2, K5 2/2. Unstable (rho<0.10, position_bias): K3 0/2, K5 1/2. Shows K_eff signal emerges at K≥5 even for stable attacks.\n\n## Robustness & Causal Identification (Section 5 / Appendix)\n\n- **sensitivity_analysis_poolA.json**: Cinelli & Hazlett (2020) OVB analysis. Pool A (9 capability-matched models). K3: 84 panels, K5: 126 panels. RV exceeds eta partial R² in **9/24** conditions (K3: 3/12, K5: 6/12). K_eff effect survives confounding up to RV level in those conditions. Strongest: arc_verbosity_bias K5 (RV=0.432).\n\n- **cross_benchmark_iv.json**: Cross-benchmark IV analysis. Exclusion restriction **violated** (K_eff structurally determined by panel composition, rho=0.76-0.78 across benchmarks). Reduced-form: K_eff significant in **34/36** conditions. 2SLS first-stage F=820-9232 (strong instruments), but 2SLS **invalid** for causal claims. Serves as robustness evidence that K_eff captures genuine panel property, not benchmark artifact.\n\n- **temperature_pilot_summary.json**: Temperature pilot (3 models × 4 temps × 2 benchmarks). K_eff delta max=**0.033** across temperatures — temperature is **NOT** a valid instrument (fails relevance). Accuracy changes ≤0.85%. ASR largely stable across temperatures. Includes MI details and cost ($4.93). 216 checkpoints loaded.\n\n- **random_fault_contrast_corrected.json**: V1→V2 corrected. RF vs adaptive attack contrast. RF R²=**0.70** >> adaptive R²=**0.28** (2.5x gap). K_eff explains much more variance under independent (random) faults vs adversarial attacks. Gap narrowed slightly after correction (0.449→0.430) but qualitatively unchanged. K_eff bootstrap sig 2/2 for RF.\n\n## Supplementary Analyses\n\n- **stochastic_dominance_analysis.json**: 15 judges, 3 tiers (frontier/mid/lightweight). 105 unordered pairs: 61 dominant (58.1%), 44 non-dominant (41.9%). Within-tier: **67.7%** non-dominant. Cross-tier: 31.1% non-dominant. Within-tier judges are more likely to be non-dominated — supports diversity argument.\n\n- **power_analysis_all_K.json**: Cluster-robust power analysis (1000 simulations). MDE@80%: K3=**0.144**, K5=**0.048**, K7=**0.036** (standardized beta). K3 power for β_std=0.1 is 64.6%; K5 achieves 84.5% at β_std=0.05. NOTE: these are V1 (pre-correction) OLS baselines with bootstrap CIs.\n\n## Regime Transition (Section 4.3)\n\n- **regime_transition_corrected.json**: V1→V2 corrected. Wild cluster bootstrap (B=9999, Rademacher, max-p, BH FDR 0.05). K3: K_eff sig 1→**4**, eta sig 5→3. K5: K_eff sig 7→**10**. K7: K_eff sig 5→**9**. Per-condition FDR-adjusted p-values included. 1365 filtered samples. All K sizes changed.\n\n## GAM Nonlinear Analysis (Section 4.4 / Appendix)\n\n- **gam_bootstrap_ci_k3.json**: GAM bootstrap CIs, K=3, B=9999. LinearGAM with 8 splines. 12/12 conditions positive direction. 11/12 have >98.75% positive rate (mmlu_score_manipulation lowest at 88.95%). Includes taxonomy split analysis (targeted vs shared_bias): all_targeted_positive=true, split_consistency_rate=0.0.\n\n- **gam_bootstrap_ci_k5.json**: GAM bootstrap CIs, K=5, B=9999. **12/12 conditions 100% positive rate** (all 9999 bootstraps positive). Tighter CIs than K3 due to 3003 panels. Strongest: arc_score_manipulation slope=34.9 [23.4, 49.5]. Taxonomy split: same structure as K3.\n\n- **gam_magnitude_gradient.json**: GAM slope magnitude comparison between targeted vs shared_bias attack types. K3: Kruskal-Wallis H=1.64, p=0.200. K5: H=0.64, p=0.423. **No significant difference** in GAM slope between attack categories. Position_bias slopes ~30-60x smaller due to ASR scale (0-1 vs 0-100%).\n\n## K_eff Distribution & Measurement (Section 3 / Appendix)\n\n- **keff_distribution.json**: Original K_eff distribution (uncorrected MI-based). K3: mean=1.92, sd=0.32. K5: mean=2.32, sd=0.39. K7: mean=2.54, sd=0.38. Range: K3 [1.23, 2.71], K5 [1.43, 3.55], K7 [1.59, 3.61].\n\n- **keff_distribution_corrected.json**: V1→V2 corrected K_eff (empirical via Gaussian copula MI, averaged across MMLU & ARC). K3: mean=**2.18**, sd=0.30. K5: mean=**3.35**, sd=0.42. K7: mean=**4.53**, sd=0.47. Substantially higher than uncorrected values.\n\n- **keff_stability_split.json**: K_eff significance by attack stability tier (Spearman rho). Stable attacks (rho≥0.90, 4 types): K3 K_eff sig **0/8**, K5 sig **4/8**. Moderate (rho~0.60, prompt_injection): K3 1/2, K5 2/2. Unstable (rho<0.10, position_bias): K3 0/2, K5 1/2. Shows K_eff signal emerges at K≥5 even for stable attacks.\n\n## Robustness & Causal Identification (Section 5 / Appendix)\n\n- **sensitivity_analysis_poolA.json**: Cinelli & Hazlett (2020) OVB analysis. Pool A (9 capability-matched models). K3: 84 panels, K5: 126 panels. RV exceeds eta partial R² in **9/24** conditions (K3: 3/12, K5: 6/12). K_eff effect survives confounding up to RV level in those conditions. Strongest: arc_verbosity_bias K5 (RV=0.432).\n\n- **cross_benchmark_iv.json**: Cross-benchmark IV analysis. Exclusion restriction **violated** (K_eff structurally determined by panel composition, rho=0.76-0.78 across benchmarks). Reduced-form: K_eff significant in **34/36** conditions. 2SLS first-stage F=820-9232 (strong instruments), but 2SLS **invalid** for causal claims. Serves as robustness evidence that K_eff captures genuine panel property, not benchmark artifact.\n\n- **temperature_pilot_summary.json**: Temperature pilot (3 models × 4 temps × 2 benchmarks). K_eff delta max=**0.033** across temperatures — temperature is **NOT** a valid instrument (fails relevance). Accuracy changes ≤0.85%. ASR largely stable across temperatures. Includes MI details and cost ($4.93). 216 checkpoints loaded.\n\n- **random_fault_contrast_corrected.json**: V1→V2 corrected. RF vs adaptive attack contrast. RF R²=**0.70** >> adaptive R²=**0.28** (2.5x gap). K_eff explains much more variance under independent (random) faults vs adversarial attacks. Gap narrowed slightly after correction (0.449→0.430) but qualitatively unchanged. K_eff bootstrap sig 2/2 for RF.\n\n## Supplementary Analyses\n\n- **stochastic_dominance_analysis.json**: 15 judges, 3 tiers (frontier 4/mid 2/lightweight 9, matching paper Table 1). 105 unordered pairs: 61 dominant (58.1%), 44 non-dominant (41.9%). Within-tier: **72.1%** non-dominant (31/43). Cross-tier: 21.0% non-dominant (13/62). Within-tier judges are more likely to be non-dominated — supports diversity argument. (Updated 2026-05-24: tier scheme unified with paper Table 1.)\n\n- **power_analysis_all_K.json**: Cluster-robust power analysis (1000 simulations). MDE@80%: K3=**0.144**, K5=**0.048**, K7=**0.036** (standardized beta). K3 power for β_std=0.1 is 64.6%; K5 achieves 84.5% at β_std=0.05. Demonstrates adequate power for observed effect sizes.\n\n---\n\n## Cross-reference: Paper Section → Data File\n\n| Section | Data File | Key Numbers |\n|---------|-----------|-------------|\n| 3 (Setup/K_eff) | keff_distribution.json | K3 mean=1.92, K5=2.32, K7=2.54 |\n| 3 (Setup/K_eff) | keff_distribution_corrected.json | K3 mean=2.18, K5=3.35, K7=4.53 |\n| 4.1 (Table 1) | table1_corrected.json | K_eff 12/12 sig, Co-dom 10/12 |\n| 4.1 (Table 1) | epsilon_sensitivity.json | K3 coefficient stability across ε |\n| 4.1 (Table 1) | epsilon_sensitivity_k5.json | K5 max K_eff change 184.5% |\n| 4.2 (Table 2) | table2_corrected.json | Majority K_eff 12/12, Bayesian dir flip |\n| 4.2 (Table 2) | aggregation_bootstrap.json | Bootstrap confirms Table 2 directions |\n| 4.3 (Regime) | regime_transition_corrected.json | K3→K5: K_eff sig 4→10, eta→0 |\n| 4.4 (GAM) | gam_bootstrap_ci_k3.json | 12/12 positive, 11/12 >98.75% rate |\n| 4.4 (GAM) | gam_bootstrap_ci_k5.json | 12/12 at 100% positive rate |\n| 4.4 (GAM) | gam_magnitude_gradient.json | No targeted/shared difference (p>0.2) |\n| 5 (Robustness) | sensitivity_analysis_poolA.json | RV > eta in 9/24 conditions |\n| 5 (Robustness) | cross_benchmark_iv.json | 34/36 reduced-form sig, 2SLS invalid |\n| 5 (Robustness) | temperature_pilot_summary.json | K_eff Δ=0.033, not valid instrument |\n| 5 (Robustness) | random_fault_contrast_corrected.json | RF R²=0.70 >> adaptive R²=0.28 |\n| 5 (Robustness) | keff_stability_split.json | Stable attacks: K3 0/8, K5 4/8 sig |\n| Appendix | stochastic_dominance_analysis.json | 41.9% non-dominant, within-tier 67.7% |\n| Appendix | power_analysis_all_K.json | MDE@80%: K3=0.14, K5=0.05, K7=0.04 |\n\n## V1→V2 Correction Summary\n\n| File | What Changed | Impact on Claims |\n|------|-------------|-----------------|\n| table1_corrected | K_eff 8/12→12/12 sig, eta-dom eliminated | Strengthens: diversity universally significant |\n| table2_corrected | Majority/weighted K_eff→12/12; Bayesian dir flip | Strengthens: error artifact removed |\n| regime_transition_corrected | K_eff sig counts increase at all K | Strengthens: regime transition more pronounced |\n| keff_distribution_corrected | K_eff means increase (1.92→2.18 at K3) | Changes magnitude, not direction |\n| random_fault_contrast_corrected | Gap narrows slightly (0.449→0.430) | Qualitatively unchanged |\n",
